Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. It sounds irrational when I say it out loud, but in the moment that’s what I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, mostly that. I mean, a few times after those late-night eating spells I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food was going to show on me.\n\nI don’t really have some whole system, you know. It’s more like I panic afterward. Funny, I can keep receipts and ledgers straight for years, but with this I don’t think straight at all. I don’t know that I do it in that organized a way. Like, I’m not waking up with a plan written down—“skip breakfast, walk five miles,” that kind of thing.

Sometimes my eating is off the next day anyway, but it’s hard to say if that’s me trying to make up for it or just feeling sort of ashamed and not wanting to think about food. Exercise… no, not really. I mean, I’ll tell myself I should walk more, but then I get caught up with little errands or paperwork, and at my age my knees aren’t exactly thrilled with big ideas. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.

methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s the weight thing, mostly. Like if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach, and then my head starts going.\n\nI start thinking people will notice, like in class they’ll look at me and think I’m just some sloppy old guy who let himself go. It’s not even just guilt, it’s like—once I start on those snacks, I keep telling myself, “Just close the bag, go to bed,” you know? I’ll even fold the top over like I’m done, and then somehow my hand’s back in there.\n\nAnd then afterward I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself. Like, I’m a grown man, I work with numbers all day, I should be able to count out a normal amount of chips or whatever, but I don’t. It’s a lousy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, close the bag, go to bed,” and I’ll even mean it. But then my hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really making the decision anymore.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, because I can sit at work and catch a ten-cent error in a ledger, you know, but with a bag of snacks I can’t seem to stop at a normal amount. By the time I do stop, I’m embarrassed and feel kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say it’s been about once a week, usually. And then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week, yeah. It’s… it’s mostly been one night after class, for the last three months or so. Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s the eating that’s been bothering me. It’s not every minute of the day, but after my evening class—I've been taking a class after work, which is a whole thing at my age, sitting with people half my age—but anyway, sometimes afterward I stop for food and it turns into more than I meant. Like a whole fast-food meal, then candy from the vending machine, and then I still go home and eat more snacks, like I’m making up for the whole day or something. It feels kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even just guilt, it’s like—once I start on those snacks, I keep telling myself, “Just close the bag, go to bed,” you know? I’ll even fold the top over like I’m done, and then somehow my hand’s back in there.\n\nAnd then afterward I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself. Like, I’m a grown man, I work with numbers all day, I should be able to count out a normal amount of chips or whatever, but I don’t. It’s a lousy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, close the bag, go to bed,” and I’ll even mean it. But then my hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really making the decision anymore.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, because I can sit at work and catch a ten-cent error in a ledger, you know, but with a bag of snacks I can’t seem to stop at a normal amount. By the time I do stop, I’m embarrassed and feel kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say it’s been about once a week, usually. Mostly one night after class. I keep thinking it’s just because those nights run late and I’m tired, and there’s that fast-food place right near the bus stop—well, not the bus, I usually drive, but anyway—it’s kind of become a pattern.\n\nFor about the last three months, there’s usually been at least one night each week where I eat like that… more than I meant to. And then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week, yeah. It’s… it’s mostly been one night after class, for the last three months or so. I’ll overeat, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI keep telling myself it’s just that the class runs late and I’m tired, and then I get home and everything’s off schedule, but… yeah, it’s been pretty regular. At least one night most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, especially at my age, because it sounds vain or something. But if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach.\n\nThen I start thinking, you know, people in class are going to look at me and think I’m just this sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. It sounds irrational when I say it out loud, but in the moment that’s what I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, mostly that. I mean, a few times after those late-night eating spells I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food was going to show on me.\n\nI don’t really have some whole system, you know. It’s more like I panic afterward. Funny, I can keep receipts and ledgers straight for years, but with this I don’t think straight at all. It’s just that fear that it’s going to turn into weight or look obvious on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more or skip meals the next day to try to undo the calories from these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in that organized a way. Like, I’m not waking up with a plan written down—“skip breakfast, walk five miles,” that kind of thing.\n\nSometimes my eating is off the next day anyway, but it’s hard to say if that’s me trying to make up for it or just feeling sort of ashamed and not wanting to think about food. Exercise… no, not really. I mean, I’ll tell myself I should walk more, but then I get caught up with little errands or paperwork, and at my age my knees aren’t exactly thrilled with big ideas. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, other than throwing up, I don’t really think of myself as using “methods,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more careful the next day, but then the day gets away from me—bills, phone calls, little things around the house. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s the eating that’s been bothering me. It’s not every minute of the day, but after my evening class—I've been taking a class after work, which is a whole thing at my age, sitting with people half my age—but anyway, sometimes afterward I stop for food and it turns into more than I meant. Like a whole fast-food meal, then candy from the vending machine, and then I still go home and eat more snacks, like I’m making up for the whole day or something. It feels kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even just guilt, it’s like—once I start on those snacks, I keep telling myself, “Just close the bag, go to bed,” you know? I’ll even fold the top over like I’m done, and then somehow my hand’s back in there.\n\nAnd then afterward I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself. Like, I’m a grown man, I work with numbers all day, I should be able to count out a normal amount of chips or whatever, but I don’t. It’s a lousy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, close the bag, go to bed,” and I’ll even mean it. But then my hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really making the decision anymore.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, because I can sit at work and catch a ten-cent error in a ledger, you know, but with a bag of snacks I can’t seem to stop at a normal amount. By the time I do stop, I’m embarrassed and feel kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say it’s been about once a week, usually. Mostly one night after class. I keep thinking it’s just because those nights run late and I’m tired, and there’s that fast-food place right near the bus stop—well, not the bus, I usually drive, but anyway—it’s kind of become a pattern.\n\nFor about the last three months, there’s usually been at least one night each week where I eat like that… more than I meant to. And then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week, yeah. It’s… it’s mostly been one night after class, for the last three months or so. I’ll overeat, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI keep telling myself it’s just that the class runs late and I’m tired, and then I get home and everything’s off schedule, but… yeah, it’s been pretty regular. At least one night most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, especially at my age, because it sounds vain or something. But if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach.\n\nThen I start thinking, you know, people in class are going to look at me and think I’m just this sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. It sounds irrational when I say it out loud, but in the moment that’s what I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, mostly that. I mean, a few times after those late-night eating spells I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food was going to show on me.\n\nI don’t really have some whole system, you know. It’s more like I panic afterward. Funny, I can keep receipts and ledgers straight for years, but with this I don’t think straight at all. It’s just that fear that it’s going to turn into weight or look obvious on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more or skip meals the next day to try to undo the calories from these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in that organized a way. Like, I’m not waking up with a plan written down—“skip breakfast, walk five miles,” that kind of thing.\n\nSometimes my eating is off the next day anyway, but it’s hard to say if that’s me trying to make up for it or just feeling sort of ashamed and not wanting to think about food. Exercise… no, not really. I mean, I’ll tell myself I should walk more, but then I get caught up with little errands or paperwork, and at my age my knees aren’t exactly thrilled with big ideas. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, other than throwing up, I don’t really think of myself as using “methods,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more careful the next day, but then the day gets away from me—bills, phone calls, little things around the house. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s the weight thing, mostly. Like if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach, and then my head starts going.\n\nI start thinking people will notice, like in class they’ll look at me and think I’m just some sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably silly—I mean, people are usually worried about their own lives, not standing there auditing me like I’m one of my old ledgers—but it feels real in the moment. So yes, I’d say that fear is a big part of why I try to get rid of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll start out thinking, “Just a few crackers,” or whatever snack is there, and then it’s like the sensible part of me is standing off to the side saying, “Close the bag and go to bed already.”\n\nBut I don’t. My hands just keep going back in, again and again. It’s ridiculous, because I used to be very exact with things—numbers, receipts, everything had a column—but with that, there’s no order to it. I just keep eating until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself. So… no, I don’t feel very in control when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you meant to been happening for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. I think it’s been about five months now.\n\nIt started around when I began those evening Certified Bookkeeper classes. Funny, I thought taking the classes would give me more structure—like getting back into ledgers and rules and things lining up neatly—but the evenings got strange somehow. I’d come home wound up, and that’s when it started happening more.\n\nSo, yes, about five months.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up or use other methods to undo calories more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. But yes, more than once a week sometimes, and at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel a sense of relief after making yourself throw up following one of these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… not relief like, “Oh, everything’s fine now,” but more like I’m less panicked for a few minutes.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food would show on me. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, other than throwing up, I don’t really think of myself as using “methods,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more careful the next day, but then the day gets away from me—bills, phone calls, little things around the house. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s the weight thing, mostly. Like if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach, and then my head starts going.\n\nI start thinking people will notice, like in class they’ll look at me and think I’m just some sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably silly—I mean, people are usually worried about their own lives, not standing there auditing me like I’m one of my old ledgers—but it feels real in the moment. So yes, I’d say that fear is a big part of why I try to get rid of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll start out thinking, “Just a few crackers,” or whatever snack is there, and then it’s like the sensible part of me is standing off to the side saying, “Close the bag and go to bed already.”\n\nBut I don’t. My hands just keep going back in, again and again. It’s ridiculous, because I used to be very exact with things—numbers, receipts, everything had a column—but with that, there’s no order to it. I just keep eating until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself. So… no, I don’t feel very in control when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you meant to been happening for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. I think it’s been about five months now.\n\nIt started around when I began those evening Certified Bookkeeper classes. Funny, I thought taking the classes would give me more structure—like getting back into ledgers and rules and things lining up neatly—but the evenings got strange somehow. I’d come home wound up, and that’s when it started happening more.\n\nSo, yes, about five months.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up or use other methods to undo calories more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not every single time I eat too much, but for about the past three months there’s usually been at least one night a week—often after class—where I come home, overdo it with food, and then I make myself throw up afterward.\n\nIt’s strange, because the class is only bookkeeping, you know, nothing dramatic, but I come home all keyed up thinking about balances and exercises and whether I’m the oldest one in the room… and then the night kind of goes off the rails. But yes, more than once a week sometimes, and at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel a sense of relief after making yourself throw up following one of these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… not relief like, “Oh, everything’s fine now,” but more like I’m less panicked for a few minutes.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food would show on me. Which sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one night isn’t supposed to change everything, but in the moment it feels like it will. Then afterward I’m just… tired and ashamed, mostly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… usually once I start, it feels like something takes over a bit. I’ll tell myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” because it’s late and I’ve got work in the morning, and I’ll even fold the top over like that’s the end of it.

It’s a lousy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, close the bag, go to bed,” and I’ll even mean it. But then my hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really making the decision anymore.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, because I can sit at work and catch a ten-cent error in a ledger, you know, but with a bag of snacks I can’t seem to stop at a normal amount. By the time I do stop, I’m embarrassed and feel kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say it’s been about once a week, usually. Mostly one night after class. I keep thinking it’s just because those nights run late and I’m tired, and there’s that fast-food place right near the bus stop—well, not the bus, I usually drive, but anyway—it’s kind of become a pattern.\n\nFor about the last three months, there’s usually been at least one night each week where I eat like that… more than I meant to. And then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week, yeah. It’s… it’s mostly been one night after class, for the last three months or so. I’ll overeat, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI keep telling myself it’s just that the class runs late and I’m tired, and then I get home and everything’s off schedule, but… yeah, it’s been pretty regular. At least one night most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, especially at my age, because it sounds vain or something. But if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach.\n\nThen I start thinking, you know, people in class are going to look at me and think I’m just this sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. It sounds irrational when I say it out loud, but in the moment that’s what I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, mostly that. I mean, a few times after those late-night eating spells I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food was going to show on me.\n\nI don’t really have some whole system, you know. It’s more like I panic afterward. Funny, I can keep receipts and ledgers straight for years, but with this I don’t think straight at all. It’s just that fear that it’s going to turn into weight or look obvious on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more or skip meals the next day to try to undo the calories from these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in that organized a way. Like, I’m not waking up with a plan written down—“skip breakfast, walk five miles,” that kind of thing.\n\nSometimes my eating is off the next day anyway, but it’s hard to say if that’s me trying to make up for it or just feeling sort of ashamed and not wanting to think about food. Exercise… no, not really. I mean, I’ll tell myself I should walk more, but then I get caught up with little errands or paperwork, and at my age my knees aren’t exactly thrilled with big ideas. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, other than throwing up, I don’t really think of myself as using “methods,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more careful the next day, but then the day gets away from me—bills, phone calls, little things around the house. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s the weight thing, mostly. Like if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach, and then my head starts going.\n\nI start thinking people will notice, like in class they’ll look at me and think I’m just some sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably silly—I mean, people are usually worried about their own lives, not standing there auditing me like I’m one of my old ledgers—but it feels real in the moment. So yes, I’d say that fear is a big part of why I try to get rid of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll start out thinking, “Just a few crackers,” or whatever snack is there, and then it’s like the sensible part of me is standing off to the side saying, “Close the bag and go to bed already.”\n\nBut I don’t. My hands just keep going back in, again and again. It’s ridiculous, because I used to be very exact with things—numbers, receipts, everything had a column—but with that, there’s no order to it. I just keep eating until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself. So… no, I don’t feel very in control when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you meant to been happening for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. I think it’s been about five months now.\n\nIt started around when I began those evening Certified Bookkeeper classes. Funny, I thought taking the classes would give me more structure—like getting back into ledgers and rules and things lining up neatly—but the evenings got strange somehow. I’d come home wound up, and that’s when it started happening more.\n\nSo, yes, about five months.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up or use other methods to undo calories more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not every single time I eat too much, but for about the past three months there’s usually been at least one night a week—often after class—where I come home, overdo it with food, and then I make myself throw up afterward.\n\nIt’s strange, because the class is only bookkeeping, you know, nothing dramatic, but I come home all keyed up thinking about balances and exercises and whether I’m the oldest one in the room… and then the night kind of goes off the rails. But yes, more than once a week sometimes, and at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel a sense of relief after making yourself throw up following one of these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… not relief like, “Oh, everything’s fine now,” but more like I’m less panicked for a few minutes.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food would show on me. Which sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one night isn’t supposed to change everything, but in the moment it feels like it will. Then afterward I’m just… tired and ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong urge to eat when you start these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually once I start, it feels like something takes over a bit. I’ll tell myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” because it’s late and I’ve got work in the morning, and I’ll even fold the top over like that’s the end of it.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back in. Chips, crackers, whatever’s there. It’s ridiculous, because I’ll be thinking about something completely different too, like whether I entered something wrong in one of those practice ledgers, and then suddenly I realize I’m still eating.\n\nSo yes, there’s an urge, and once it starts I don’t feel very good at stopping. I keep going until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Which sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one night isn’t supposed to change everything, but in the moment it feels like it will. Then afterward I’m just… tired and ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong urge to eat when you start these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually once I start, it feels like something takes over a bit. By the time I do stop, I’m embarrassed and feel kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say it’s been about once a week, usually. Mostly one night after class. I keep thinking it’s just because those nights run late and I’m tired, and there’s that fast-food place right near the bus stop—well, not the bus, I usually drive, but anyway—it’s kind of become a pattern.\n\nFor about the last three months, there’s usually been at least one night each week where I eat like that… more than I meant to. And then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week, yeah. It’s… it’s mostly been one night after class, for the last three months or so. I’ll overeat, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI keep telling myself it’s just that the class runs late and I’m tired, and then I get home and everything’s off schedule, but… yeah, it’s been pretty regular. At least one night most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, especially at my age, because it sounds vain or something. But if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach.\n\nThen I start thinking, you know, people in class are going to look at me and think I’m just this sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. It sounds irrational when I say it out loud, but in the moment that’s what I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, mostly that. I mean, a few times after those late-night eating spells I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food was going to show on me.\n\nI don’t really have some whole system, you know. It’s more like I panic afterward. Funny, I can keep receipts and ledgers straight for years, but with this I don’t think straight at all. It’s just that fear that it’s going to turn into weight or look obvious on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more or skip meals the next day to try to undo the calories from these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in that organized a way. Like, I’m not waking up with a plan written down—“skip breakfast, walk five miles,” that kind of thing.\n\nSometimes my eating is off the next day anyway, but it’s hard to say if that’s me trying to make up for it or just feeling sort of ashamed and not wanting to think about food. Exercise… no, not really. I mean, I’ll tell myself I should walk more, but then I get caught up with little errands or paperwork, and at my age my knees aren’t exactly thrilled with big ideas. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, other than throwing up, I don’t really think of myself as using “methods,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more careful the next day, but then the day gets away from me—bills, phone calls, little things around the house. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s the weight thing, mostly. Like if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach, and then my head starts going.\n\nI start thinking people will notice, like in class they’ll look at me and think I’m just some sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably silly—I mean, people are usually worried about their own lives, not standing there auditing me like I’m one of my old ledgers—but it feels real in the moment. So yes, I’d say that fear is a big part of why I try to get rid of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll start out thinking, “Just a few crackers,” or whatever snack is there, and then it’s like the sensible part of me is standing off to the side saying, “Close the bag and go to bed already.”\n\nBut I don’t. My hands just keep going back in, again and again. It’s ridiculous, because I used to be very exact with things—numbers, receipts, everything had a column—but with that, there’s no order to it. I just keep eating until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself. So… no, I don’t feel very in control when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you meant to been happening for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. I think it’s been about five months now.\n\nIt started around when I began those evening Certified Bookkeeper classes. Funny, I thought taking the classes would give me more structure—like getting back into ledgers and rules and things lining up neatly—but the evenings got strange somehow. I’d come home wound up, and that’s when it started happening more.\n\nSo, yes, about five months.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up or use other methods to undo calories more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not every single time I eat too much, but for about the past three months there’s usually been at least one night a week—often after class—where I come home, overdo it with food, and then I make myself throw up afterward.\n\nIt’s strange, because the class is only bookkeeping, you know, nothing dramatic, but I come home all keyed up thinking about balances and exercises and whether I’m the oldest one in the room… and then the night kind of goes off the rails. But yes, more than once a week sometimes, and at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel a sense of relief after making yourself throw up following one of these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… not relief like, “Oh, everything’s fine now,” but more like I’m less panicked for a few minutes.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food would show on me. Which sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one night isn’t supposed to change everything, but in the moment it feels like it will. Then afterward I’m just… tired and ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong urge to eat when you start these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually once I start, it feels like something takes over a bit. I’ll tell myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” because it’s late and I’ve got work in the morning, and I’ll even fold the top over like that’s the end of it.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back in. Chips, crackers, whatever’s there. It’s ridiculous, because I’ll be thinking about something completely different too, like whether I entered something wrong in one of those practice ledgers, and then suddenly I realize I’m still eating.\n\nSo yes, there’s an urge, and once it starts I don’t feel very good at stopping. I keep going until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up more than once a week after eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more than once some weeks. I know I sort of said this already, but it’s been going on for about three months.\n\nUsually there’s at least one night a week after my class where I come home, eat way more than I meant to, and then I make myself throw up afterward. Sometimes I tell myself it was just because the evening got away from me, or I was thinking about class stuff, but… yes, it’s been at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a week on average?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s twice a week on average, no. It’s usually… like, about once a week. For the past three months or so, there’s usually been at least one night after class where I come home, eat too much, and then end up making myself throw up.\n\nSome weeks feel worse than others, but if you’re asking average, I’d say more like weekly, not twice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s ridiculous, because I’ll be thinking about something completely different too, like whether I entered something wrong in one of those practice ledgers, and then suddenly I realize I’m still eating.\n\nSo yes, there’s an urge, and once it starts I don’t feel very good at stopping. I keep going until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up more than once a week after eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more than once some weeks. I know I sort of said this already, but it’s been going on for about three months.\n\nUsually there’s at least one night a week after my class where I come home, eat way more than I meant to, and then I make myself throw up afterward. Sometimes I tell myself it was just because the evening got away from me, or I was thinking about class stuff, but… yes, it’s been at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a week on average?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s twice a week on average, no. It’s usually… like, about once a week. For the past three months or so, there’s usually been at least one night after class where I come home, eat too much, and then end up making myself throw up.\n\nSome weeks feel worse than others, but if you’re asking average, I’d say more like weekly, not twice.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a month, but not necessarily every week?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"For about the past three months, there has usually been at least one night each week after class when I overeat like that and then make myself throw up afterward.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, it’s more than twice a month. It’s usually about once a week, honestly.

For the past three months or so, there’s usually been one night each week after class where I come home, eat too much, and then make myself throw up afterward. I keep thinking it’s tied to those class nights, like I get home late and my head’s still on numbers and assignments, and then the whole evening just sort of goes sideways. But yes—at least twice a month, definitely.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s the eating that’s been bothering me. It’s not every minute of the day, but after my evening class—I've been taking a class after work, which is a whole thing at my age, sitting with people half my age—but anyway, sometimes afterward I stop for food and it turns into more than I meant. Like a whole fast-food meal, then candy from the vending machine, and then I still go home and eat more snacks, like I’m making up for the whole day or something. It feels kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even just guilt, it’s like—once I start on those snacks, I keep telling myself, “Just close the bag, go to bed,” you know? I’ll even fold the top over like I’m done, and then somehow my hand’s back in there.\n\nAnd then afterward I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself. Like, I’m a grown man, I work with numbers all day, I should be able to count out a normal amount of chips or whatever, but I don’t. It’s a lousy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, close the bag, go to bed,” and I’ll even mean it. But then my hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really making the decision anymore.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, because I can sit at work and catch a ten-cent error in a ledger, you know, but with a bag of snacks I can’t seem to stop at a normal amount. By the time I do stop, I’m embarrassed and feel kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say it’s been about once a week, usually. Mostly one night after class. I keep thinking it’s just because those nights run late and I’m tired, and there’s that fast-food place right near the bus stop—well, not the bus, I usually drive, but anyway—it’s kind of become a pattern.\n\nFor about the last three months, there’s usually been at least one night each week where I eat like that… more than I meant to. And then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week, yeah. It’s… it’s mostly been one night after class, for the last three months or so. I’ll overeat, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI keep telling myself it’s just that the class runs late and I’m tired, and then I get home and everything’s off schedule, but… yeah, it’s been pretty regular. At least one night most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, especially at my age, because it sounds vain or something. But if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach.\n\nThen I start thinking, you know, people in class are going to look at me and think I’m just this sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. It sounds irrational when I say it out loud, but in the moment that’s what I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, mostly that. I mean, a few times after those late-night eating spells I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food was going to show on me.\n\nI don’t really have some whole system, you know. It’s more like I panic afterward. Funny, I can keep receipts and ledgers straight for years, but with this I don’t think straight at all. It’s just that fear that it’s going to turn into weight or look obvious on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more or skip meals the next day to try to undo the calories from these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in that organized a way. Like, I’m not waking up with a plan written down—“skip breakfast, walk five miles,” that kind of thing.\n\nSometimes my eating is off the next day anyway, but it’s hard to say if that’s me trying to make up for it or just feeling sort of ashamed and not wanting to think about food. Exercise… no, not really. I mean, I’ll tell myself I should walk more, but then I get caught up with little errands or paperwork, and at my age my knees aren’t exactly thrilled with big ideas. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, other than throwing up, I don’t really think of myself as using “methods,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more careful the next day, but then the day gets away from me—bills, phone calls, little things around the house. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s the weight thing, mostly. Like if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach, and then my head starts going.\n\nI start thinking people will notice, like in class they’ll look at me and think I’m just some sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably silly—I mean, people are usually worried about their own lives, not standing there auditing me like I’m one of my old ledgers—but it feels real in the moment. So yes, I’d say that fear is a big part of why I try to get rid of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll start out thinking, “Just a few crackers,” or whatever snack is there, and then it’s like the sensible part of me is standing off to the side saying, “Close the bag and go to bed already.”\n\nBut I don’t. My hands just keep going back in, again and again. It’s ridiculous, because I used to be very exact with things—numbers, receipts, everything had a column—but with that, there’s no order to it. I just keep eating until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself. So… no, I don’t feel very in control when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you meant to been happening for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. I think it’s been about five months now.\n\nIt started around when I began those evening Certified Bookkeeper classes. Funny, I thought taking the classes would give me more structure—like getting back into ledgers and rules and things lining up neatly—but the evenings got strange somehow. I’d come home wound up, and that’s when it started happening more.\n\nSo, yes, about five months.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up or use other methods to undo calories more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not every single time I eat too much, but for about the past three months there’s usually been at least one night a week—often after class—where I come home, overdo it with food, and then I make myself throw up afterward.\n\nIt’s strange, because the class is only bookkeeping, you know, nothing dramatic, but I come home all keyed up thinking about balances and exercises and whether I’m the oldest one in the room… and then the night kind of goes off the rails. But yes, more than once a week sometimes, and at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel a sense of relief after making yourself throw up following one of these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… not relief like, “Oh, everything’s fine now,” but more like I’m less panicked for a few minutes.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food would show on me. Which sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one night isn’t supposed to change everything, but in the moment it feels like it will. Then afterward I’m just… tired and ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong urge to eat when you start these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually once I start, it feels like something takes over a bit. I’ll tell myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” because it’s late and I’ve got work in the morning, and I’ll even fold the top over like that’s the end of it.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back in. Chips, crackers, whatever’s there. It’s ridiculous, because I’ll be thinking about something completely different too, like whether I entered something wrong in one of those practice ledgers, and then suddenly I realize I’m still eating.\n\nSo yes, there’s an urge, and once it starts I don’t feel very good at stopping. Not twice a week, no… I don’t think so. It’s more like, for about the past three months, there’s usually been one night each week—often after class—where I come home, eat way too much, and then make myself throw up afterward.

I know I keep circling back to the class thing, but that’s when it tends to happen. So, weekly, yes… twice a week for three months, not really.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s the eating that’s been bothering me. It’s not every minute of the day, but after my evening class—I've been taking a class after work, which is a whole thing at my age, sitting with people half my age—but anyway, sometimes afterward I stop for food and it turns into more than I meant. Like a whole fast-food meal, then candy from the vending machine, and then I still go home and eat more snacks, like I’m making up for the whole day or something. It feels kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even just guilt, it’s like—once I start on those snacks, I keep telling myself, “Just close the bag, go to bed,” you know? I’ll even fold the top over like I’m done, and then somehow my hand’s back in there.\n\nAnd then afterward I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself. Like, I’m a grown man, I work with numbers all day, I should be able to count out a normal amount of chips or whatever, but I don’t. It’s a lousy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, close the bag, go to bed,” and I’ll even mean it. But then my hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really making the decision anymore.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, because I can sit at work and catch a ten-cent error in a ledger, you know, but with a bag of snacks I can’t seem to stop at a normal amount. By the time I do stop, I’m embarrassed and feel kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say it’s been about once a week, usually. Mostly one night after class. I keep thinking it’s just because those nights run late and I’m tired, and there’s that fast-food place right near the bus stop—well, not the bus, I usually drive, but anyway—it’s kind of become a pattern.\n\nFor about the last three months, there’s usually been at least one night each week where I eat like that… more than I meant to. And then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week, yeah. It’s… it’s mostly been one night after class, for the last three months or so. I’ll overeat, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI keep telling myself it’s just that the class runs late and I’m tired, and then I get home and everything’s off schedule, but… yeah, it’s been pretty regular. At least one night most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, especially at my age, because it sounds vain or something. But if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach.\n\nThen I start thinking, you know, people in class are going to look at me and think I’m just this sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. It sounds irrational when I say it out loud, but in the moment that’s what I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, mostly that. I mean, a few times after those late-night eating spells I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food was going to show on me.\n\nI don’t really have some whole system, you know. It’s more like I panic afterward. Funny, I can keep receipts and ledgers straight for years, but with this I don’t think straight at all. It’s just that fear that it’s going to turn into weight or look obvious on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more or skip meals the next day to try to undo the calories from these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in that organized a way. Like, I’m not waking up with a plan written down—“skip breakfast, walk five miles,” that kind of thing.\n\nSometimes my eating is off the next day anyway, but it’s hard to say if that’s me trying to make up for it or just feeling sort of ashamed and not wanting to think about food. Exercise… no, not really. I mean, I’ll tell myself I should walk more, but then I get caught up with little errands or paperwork, and at my age my knees aren’t exactly thrilled with big ideas. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, other than throwing up, I don’t really think of myself as using “methods,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more careful the next day, but then the day gets away from me—bills, phone calls, little things around the house. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s the weight thing, mostly. Like if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach, and then my head starts going.\n\nI start thinking people will notice, like in class they’ll look at me and think I’m just some sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably silly—I mean, people are usually worried about their own lives, not standing there auditing me like I’m one of my old ledgers—but it feels real in the moment. So yes, I’d say that fear is a big part of why I try to get rid of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll start out thinking, “Just a few crackers,” or whatever snack is there, and then it’s like the sensible part of me is standing off to the side saying, “Close the bag and go to bed already.”\n\nBut I don’t. My hands just keep going back in, again and again. It’s ridiculous, because I used to be very exact with things—numbers, receipts, everything had a column—but with that, there’s no order to it. I just keep eating until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself. So… no, I don’t feel very in control when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you meant to been happening for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. I think it’s been about five months now.\n\nIt started around when I began those evening Certified Bookkeeper classes. Funny, I thought taking the classes would give me more structure—like getting back into ledgers and rules and things lining up neatly—but the evenings got strange somehow. I’d come home wound up, and that’s when it started happening more.\n\nSo, yes, about five months.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up or use other methods to undo calories more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not every single time I eat too much, but for about the past three months there’s usually been at least one night a week—often after class—where I come home, overdo it with food, and then I make myself throw up afterward.\n\nIt’s strange, because the class is only bookkeeping, you know, nothing dramatic, but I come home all keyed up thinking about balances and exercises and whether I’m the oldest one in the room… and then the night kind of goes off the rails. But yes, more than once a week sometimes, and at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel a sense of relief after making yourself throw up following one of these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… not relief like, “Oh, everything’s fine now,” but more like I’m less panicked for a few minutes.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food would show on me. Which sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one night isn’t supposed to change everything, but in the moment it feels like it will. Then afterward I’m just… tired and ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong urge to eat when you start these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually once I start, it feels like something takes over a bit. I’ll tell myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” because it’s late and I’ve got work in the morning, and I’ll even fold the top over like that’s the end of it.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back in. Chips, crackers, whatever’s there. It’s ridiculous, because I’ll be thinking about something completely different too, like whether I entered something wrong in one of those practice ledgers, and then suddenly I realize I’m still eating.\n\nSo yes, there’s an urge, and once it starts I don’t feel very good at stopping. I keep going until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up more than once a week after eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more than once some weeks. I know I sort of said this already, but it’s been going on for about three months.\n\nUsually there’s at least one night a week after my class where I come home, eat way more than I meant to, and then I make myself throw up afterward. Sometimes I tell myself it was just because the evening got away from me, or I was thinking about class stuff, but… yes, it’s been at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a week on average?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s twice a week on average, no. It’s usually… like, about once a week. For the past three months or so, there’s usually been at least one night after class where I come home, eat too much, and then end up making myself throw up.\n\nSome weeks feel worse than others, but if you’re asking average, I’d say more like weekly, not twice.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a month, but not necessarily every week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s more than twice a month. It’s usually about once a week, honestly.\n\nFor the past three months or so, there’s usually been one night each week after class where I come home, eat too much, and then make myself throw up afterward. I keep thinking it’s tied to those class nights, like I get home late and my head’s still on numbers and assignments, and then the whole evening just sort of goes sideways. But yes—at least twice a month, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Not twice a week, no… I don’t think so. It’s more like, for about the past three months, there’s usually been one night each week—often after class—where I come home, eat way too much, and then make myself throw up afterward.\n\nI know I keep circling back to the class thing, but that’s when it tends to happen. So, weekly, yes… twice a week for three months, not really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach.\n\nThen I start thinking, you know, people in class are going to look at me and think I’m just this sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably not even what they’re thinking—half of them are worried about their own assignments or phones or whatever—but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up solely to undo the calories from the overeating?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah. I mean, it’s not like I sit there doing math on the calories exactly—though I used to be decent with numbers, working in bookkeeping and all that—but it’s more that I get scared it’s going to show on me.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I was thinking, “I can’t have all that sitting in me.” Like it would turn into weight right away. It sounds irrational when I say it out loud, but in the moment that’s what I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, mostly that. I mean, a few times after those late-night eating spells I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food was going to show on me.\n\nI don’t really have some whole system, you know. It’s more like I panic afterward. Funny, I can keep receipts and ledgers straight for years, but with this I don’t think straight at all. It’s just that fear that it’s going to turn into weight or look obvious on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more or skip meals the next day to try to undo the calories from these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in that organized a way. Like, I’m not waking up with a plan written down—“skip breakfast, walk five miles,” that kind of thing.\n\nSometimes my eating is off the next day anyway, but it’s hard to say if that’s me trying to make up for it or just feeling sort of ashamed and not wanting to think about food. Exercise… no, not really. I mean, I’ll tell myself I should walk more, but then I get caught up with little errands or paperwork, and at my age my knees aren’t exactly thrilled with big ideas. So I wouldn’t say I really use exercise that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use methods other than making yourself throw up to try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, other than throwing up, I don’t really think of myself as using “methods,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more careful the next day, but then the day gets away from me—bills, phone calls, little things around the house. I used to be better at keeping things in neat categories when I was working, but with this it’s all kind of messy in my head.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another regular thing I do to undo it. It’s mostly just that panic afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s the weight thing, mostly. Like if I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach, and then my head starts going.\n\nI start thinking people will notice, like in class they’ll look at me and think I’m just some sloppy old guy who let himself go. Which is probably silly—I mean, people are usually worried about their own lives, not standing there auditing me like I’m one of my old ledgers—but it feels real in the moment. So yes, I’d say that fear is a big part of why I try to get rid of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll start out thinking, “Just a few crackers,” or whatever snack is there, and then it’s like the sensible part of me is standing off to the side saying, “Close the bag and go to bed already.”\n\nBut I don’t. My hands just keep going back in, again and again. It’s ridiculous, because I used to be very exact with things—numbers, receipts, everything had a column—but with that, there’s no order to it. I just keep eating until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself. So… no, I don’t feel very in control when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you meant to been happening for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. I think it’s been about five months now.\n\nIt started around when I began those evening Certified Bookkeeper classes. Funny, I thought taking the classes would give me more structure—like getting back into ledgers and rules and things lining up neatly—but the evenings got strange somehow. I’d come home wound up, and that’s when it started happening more.\n\nSo, yes, about five months.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up or use other methods to undo calories more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not every single time I eat too much, but for about the past three months there’s usually been at least one night a week—often after class—where I come home, overdo it with food, and then I make myself throw up afterward.\n\nIt’s strange, because the class is only bookkeeping, you know, nothing dramatic, but I come home all keyed up thinking about balances and exercises and whether I’m the oldest one in the room… and then the night kind of goes off the rails. But yes, more than once a week sometimes, and at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel a sense of relief after making yourself throw up following one of these episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… not relief like, “Oh, everything’s fine now,” but more like I’m less panicked for a few minutes.\n\nA few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up because I got scared all that food would show on me. Which sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one night isn’t supposed to change everything, but in the moment it feels like it will. Then afterward I’m just… tired and ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong urge to eat when you start these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually once I start, it feels like something takes over a bit. I’ll tell myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” because it’s late and I’ve got work in the morning, and I’ll even fold the top over like that’s the end of it.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back in. Chips, crackers, whatever’s there. It’s ridiculous, because I’ll be thinking about something completely different too, like whether I entered something wrong in one of those practice ledgers, and then suddenly I realize I’m still eating.\n\nSo yes, there’s an urge, and once it starts I don’t feel very good at stopping. I keep going until I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up more than once a week after eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more than once some weeks. I know I sort of said this already, but it’s been going on for about three months.\n\nUsually there’s at least one night a week after my class where I come home, eat way more than I meant to, and then I make myself throw up afterward. Sometimes I tell myself it was just because the evening got away from me, or I was thinking about class stuff, but… yes, it’s been at least weekly lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a week on average?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s twice a week on average, no. It’s usually… like, about once a week. For the past three months or so, there’s usually been at least one night after class where I come home, eat too much, and then end up making myself throw up.\n\nSome weeks feel worse than others, but if you’re asking average, I’d say more like weekly, not twice.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a month, but not necessarily every week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s more than twice a month. It’s usually about once a week, honestly.\n\nFor the past three months or so, there’s usually been one night each week after class where I come home, eat too much, and then make myself throw up afterward. I keep thinking it’s tied to those class nights, like I get home late and my head’s still on numbers and assignments, and then the whole evening just sort of goes sideways. But yes—at least twice a month, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating too much at least twice a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Not twice a week, no… I don’t think so. It’s more like, for about the past three months, there’s usually been one night each week—often after class—where I come home, eat way too much, and then make myself throw up afterward.\n\nI know I keep circling back to the class thing, but that’s when it tends to happen. So, weekly, yes… twice a week for three months, not really."
